Abstract written by a model

In Psalms: The Anointed is the target of the kings' and rulers' plotting alongside the LORD. In Psalms: David's Lord is a royal figure commanded to sit at the LORD's right hand and rule among his enemies. In Psalms: The king is the LORD's anointed, for whom worshippers pray: that he be answered in trouble, set on high, and granted his heart's desire. In Daniel (Greek): receives everlasting kingdom [dominion, glory; all peoples serve him] One like a son of man comes with the clouds and is brought near before the Ancient of Days. In The Gospel According to Matthew, Jesus is presented as the Christ, Son of David and Son of God, whose ministry, death, resurrection, and commission fulfill Scripture. In The Gospel According to Matthew: The Son of Man is a figure who will come at an unknown day and hour, so people must watch; he arrives in glory with all the holy angels and sits on his glorious throne. In The Gospel According to Matthew: The Christ is a title-bearing figure whose identity is the central question. In The Good News According to Mark, Jesus is presented as the beloved Son of God and Christ who proclaims God's Kingdom, serves, suffers, dies, rises, and commissions his followers.

and 35 more works

In The Good News According to Mark: Christ is a messianic title and figure. In Chapter 8, Peter identifies Jesus as the Christ, and Jesus commands silence about it. In The Gospel According to Luke: will_come_in a cloud [with power and great glory] Jesus says people will see the Son of Man coming in a cloud with power and great glory. In The Gospel According to Luke, Jesus is presented as the Son of God, Savior, Christ, and Lord who teaches, heals, dies, rises, and ascends into heaven. In The Gospel According to Luke: The Christ is an awaited coming figure, whom people wondered John might be; popularly called David's son, a claim Jesus questions, noting David calls him Lord, leaving the puzzle unresolved. In The Gospel According to John, Jesus is the central figure presented as the Word made flesh, the Son of God, and the Savior who is crucified, rises, and commissions his disciples. In The Acts of the Apostles, Jesus is presented as God's risen and exalted Servant, Lord, Christ, Savior, Judge, and the name through which people are saved. In Paul’s Letter to the Romans, Jesus Christ is presented as God's Son and Lord whose death and resurrection bring justification, reconciliation, salvation, and life to believers. In Paul’s First Letter to the Corinthians, Jesus Christ is presented as Lord, Son of God, crucified and risen Savior, spiritual foundation, Passover, and head of the church. In Paul’s First Letter to the Corinthians: Jesus is called the Lord, in whose name the readers were justified. In 2 Corinthians, Christ is presented as the Son of God and Lord through whom God's promises, reconciliation, grace, and resurrection life are revealed. In Paul’s Letter to the Galatians, Jesus Christ is presented as the risen Lord who gives himself for sins, redeems believers from the law's curse, and brings freedom. In Paul’s Letter to the Galatians: Son of God, Christ Jesus, was sent by God, born to a woman and under the law, to redeem those under the law and bring adoption as children; readers received him as an angel, and the author labors until Christ is formed in them. In Paul’s Letter to the Ephesians, Jesus Christ is presented as exalted Lord, head and Savior of the church, reconciler of Jews and Gentiles, and model of sacrificial love. In Paul’s Letter to the Colossians, Christ is presented as the image of the invisible God, creator and sustainer, head of the church, redeemer, and fullness of deity. In 1 Thessalonians: Lord Jesus Christ is God's Son, named with God the Father as the one in whom the assembly exists and a source of grace and peace. In 1 Timothy, Christ Jesus is presented as the man and Lord who saves sinners, mediates between God and humanity, gives himself as a ransom, and will return as King of kings. In 2 Timothy: Jesus Christ is presented as Savior and Lord, descended from David and risen from the dead. In 2 Timothy: Lord Jesus Christ is the righteous judge of the living and the dead, before whom Paul gives his charge. In Titus: Jesus Christ is described as our great God and Savior, whose glorious appearing is the believers' blessed hope. In The Letter to the Hebrews, Jesus is presented as the eternal Son and perfected high priest who brings salvation through suffering and lives forever to intercede. In The Letter to the Hebrews, Christ is presented as God's eternal Son, high priest, mediator, and author of salvation who offers himself once for all and will appear again. In 1 Peter, Jesus Christ is presented as the risen Lord, suffering servant, living stone, chief cornerstone, and Shepherd who redeems and oversees believers. In 1 John, Jesus Christ is presented as the righteous Son of God, Counselor, atoning sacrifice, Savior of the world, and source of eternal life. In 2 John: Jesus Christ is called the Lord and the Son of the Father, and is a source of grace, mercy, and peace. In Jude: Jesus Christ is the one Jude serves, and those called are kept for him. In Revelation, Christ is presented as the faithful witness, firstborn of the dead, ruler of kings, Living One, and reigning Lord who judges and shares his reign with the faithful. In Revelation, Jesus is presented as the exalted Son of God who addresses the assemblies, judges, rewards the faithful, and comes as the Alpha and Omega. In Revelation, the Lamb is presented as the slain Lion of Judah and Root of David who is worthy to open the book, receives worship, judges, shepherds, and triumphs. In Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa): Jesus son of Mary is the Messiah and Allah's messenger. Though some claim to have slain him, he is neither slain nor crucified; Allah takes him up unto Himself. In Surah 5: The Table (Al-Maaida): Jesus, son of Mary, is a messenger who eats earthly food with his mother, follows predecessors who passed away, and calls the Children of Israel to worship Allah. In Surah 5: The Table (Al-Maaida), Jesus, son of Mary, is presented as a prophet strengthened by Allah who performs signs, worships Allah, and denies being worshiped. In Surah 21: The Prophets (Al-Anbiyaa): The son of the chaste woman is made a token for all peoples. In The Believers (Al-Muminoon): The son of Mary and his mother are made a portent and are given refuge on a height with flocks and watersprings. In Surah 43: Ornaments of gold (Az-Zukhruf): Jesus, the son of Mary, is described as a slave upon whom Allah bestowed favour and as a pattern for the Children of Israel. In Surah 61: The Ranks (As-Saff): Jesus son of Mary said that he was Allah's messenger to the Children of Israel, confirming the Torah and bringing good tidings of a messenger after him.

One sentence for each work Jesus is in, in canon order. A model wrote them when it joined the works; each title opens that work’s own account below.

written by a model marks a model’s summary or wording. the source’s own words are the quotations: each one opens the source text at its passage, where it can be read in context and judged.

In each work

Jesus in each of the 43 accounts the graph joined, in canon order. Nothing is merged: an abstract, a narrative and a claim stay with the work they were written from.

Anointed (the LORD's Anointed) in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Anointed (the LORD's Anointed) first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Anointed is the target of the kings' and rulers' plotting alongside the LORD. He is identified as the King the LORD has set on Zion, and as the son to whom the LORD gives the nations as an inheritance and the earth's far reaches as a possession. He is to break the nations with a rod of iron and dash them like a potter's vessel, and the kings are told to give homage to the Son, lest his wrath be kindled; those who take refuge in him are blessed.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 2

The Anointed is the target of the kings' and rulers' plotting alongside the LORD. He is identified as the King the LORD has set on Zion, and as the son to whom the LORD gives the nations as an inheritance and the earth's far reaches as a possession. He is to break the nations with a rod of iron and dash them like a potter's vessel, and the kings are told to give homage to the Son, lest his wrath be kindled; those who take refuge in him are blessed.

my Lord (David's Lord) in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where my Lord (David's Lord) first appears

Abstract written by a model

David's Lord is a royal figure commanded to sit at the LORD's right hand and rule among his enemies. His people willingly serve him on the day of his power, and he is sworn a priest forever in the order of Melchizedek. He crushes kings in wrath, judges nations, and crushes the ruler of the whole earth. He drinks from the brook on the way and lifts up his head.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 110

David's Lord is told to sit at the LORD's right hand and to rule among his enemies. His people offer themselves willingly in holy array on the day of his power, and he is declared a priest forever in the order of Melchizedek. He will crush kings in the day of his wrath, judge among the nations, heap up dead bodies, and crush the ruler of the whole earth. He will drink from the brook on the way and therefore lift up his head.

king (the anointed) in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where king (the anointed) first appears

Abstract written by a model

The king is the LORD's anointed, for whom worshippers pray: that he be answered in trouble, set on high, and granted his heart's desire. His offerings are to be remembered; they affirm the LORD saves him, and close by calling on him.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 20

The king is the recipient of the worshippers' prayer, which asks that he be answered in trouble, set up on high, and granted his heart's desire and counsel. His offerings and burned sacrifice are to be remembered and accepted. The speakers state they now know the LORD saves him and will answer him from heaven. The psalm ends by calling on the King to answer when they call.

Jesus in The Gospel According to Matthew

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is the central figure of these records, presented as Jesus Christ: son of David and Abraham, firstborn of Mary, conceived by the Holy Spirit, named by Joseph at the angel's command, and called Immanuel, 'God with us', who will save his people from their sins. He is born in Bethlehem in the days of King Herod, called King of the Jews by the wise men, taken to Egypt, and raised in Nazareth, so that he is called a Nazarene. He comes from Galilee to be baptized by John, who is less mighty than he; the Spirit of God descends and a voice calls him beloved Son. He fasts forty days and nights, resists temptation, then settles in Capernaum, preaches repentance and the Kingdom of Heaven, teaches in synagogues, and calls Simon Peter, Andrew, James and John. His ministry shows authority: he teaches as one with authority, casts out demons, calms the storm, heals many, and forgives sins as the Son of Man. He feels compassion for the multitudes, appoints twelve disciples and sends them to the lost sheep of Israel. He is accused of gluttony and called friend of tax collectors and sinners, yet describes himself as gentle and humble in heart, giving rest to the weary. He claims a unique relationship with the Father, who has delivered all things to him. He is Lord of the Sabbath and greater than the temple, Jonah and Solomon, though rejected in his own country, where his mother, brothers and carpenter father are named. Peter confesses him as the Christ; he is transfigured, his face shining like the sun, and the voice again names him beloved Son. He foretells his death and resurrection, leaves Galilee, goes up to Jerusalem, and enters as a humble King on a donkey, acclaimed Son of David. He debates opponents, teaches on the Mount of Olives, keeps Passover with his disciples, and before the high priest affirms he is the Christ, the Son of God. He is accused as King of the Jews, crucified, and buried by Joseph of Arimathaea. The angel announces him risen. In Galilee he tells the eleven that all authority in heaven and on earth is given to him, commands them to make disciples, and promises to be with them to the end of the age.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

26 sections; each heading opens the text

The Gospel According to Matthew / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is introduced as the son of David and the son of Abraham, the one in whom the genealogy culminates, born of Mary and called Christ. He is conceived by the Holy Spirit, is to be named Jesus because he will save his people from their sins, and is called Immanuel, 'God with us'.

Chapter 2

Jesus is born in Bethlehem of Judea in the days of King Herod and is worshiped by the wise men, who offer him gold, frankincense, and myrrh. He is taken to Egypt by night to escape Herod, later brought back to the land of Israel, and raised in Nazareth, so that he will be called a Nazarene.

Chapter 3

Jesus comes from Galilee to the Jordan to be baptized by John. He tells John to allow it as the fitting way to fulfill all righteousness. After he comes up from the water, the Spirit of God descends on him and a voice from the heavens calls him beloved Son, with whom it is well pleased.

Chapter 4

Jesus is led by the Spirit into the wilderness, fasts forty days and nights, and resists the devil's three temptations by quoting Scripture. After John is delivered up, he withdraws to Galilee and lives in Capernaum, begins preaching repentance, calls four fishermen to follow him, and goes about Galilee teaching, preaching, and healing, drawing great multitudes.

Chapter 5

Jesus goes up onto the mountain, sits, and teaches his disciples, blessing the poor in spirit, the meek, the persecuted, and others. He says he came to fulfill the law and the prophets, not to destroy them, and repeatedly contrasts what was said to the ancient ones with 'But I tell you.' He commands reconciliation, no oaths, non-resistance, and love of enemies, and calls his hearers to be perfect like the Father.

Chapter 7

Jesus teaches the multitudes about judging, asking, the narrow gate, false prophets, and doing God's will. He says he will tell those who work iniquity, 'I never knew you. Depart from me.' When he finishes, the multitudes are astonished because he taught with authority, not like the scribes.

Chapter 8

Jesus cleanses a leper, heals a centurion's servant, Peter's wife's mother, and many sick people, and casts out spirits with a word. He tells would-be followers that the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head, calms the storm, and sends demons into pigs, after which the city asks him to depart from their borders.

Chapter 9

Jesus forgives a paralytic's sins and heals him, calls Matthew, eats with tax collectors and sinners, and answers the Pharisees and John's disciples with sayings about physicians, mercy, the bridegroom, and new wine. He heals the woman with the discharge of blood, raises the ruler's daughter, restores sight to two blind men, and casts a demon out of a mute man. He teaches and heals in all the cities and villages, is moved with compassion for the multitudes, and asks his disciples to pray for laborers for the harvest.

Chapter 10

Jesus calls the twelve disciples, gives them authority over unclean spirits and disease, and sends them out with detailed instructions to preach, heal, and go only to the lost sheep of Israel. He warns them of persecution, tells them not to fear, says he came to send a sword rather than peace, and demands that he be loved above family. He promises that receiving his disciples is receiving him and the one who sent him.

Chapter 11

Jesus finishes instructing the twelve and goes to teach and preach in their cities. He answers John's question by pointing to the healing and good news happening, praises John to the crowds, denounces unrepentant cities, thanks the Father, and says all things are delivered to him and that only he can reveal the Father. He then invites the weary to come to him, describing himself as gentle and humble in heart.

Chapter 12

Jesus defends his disciples' plucking of grain on the Sabbath, heals a man's withered hand, and withdraws when the Pharisees conspire against him. He heals a possessed blind and mute man, rebuts the charge that he acts by Beelzebul, and refuses the scribes and Pharisees a sign except that of Jonah. He declares himself Lord of the Sabbath, greater than the temple, Jonah, and Solomon, and names those who do his Father's will as his family.

Chapter 13

Jesus sits by the sea and teaches the multitudes in parables, then explains the sower and darnel parables privately to his disciples. He tells further parables of the mustard seed, yeast, treasure, pearl and dragnet, and in his own country he is rejected, saying a prophet is not without honor except in his own country and house.

Chapter 14

Jesus withdraws by boat to a deserted place after hearing of John's death, heals the sick among the following crowds, and feeds about five thousand men plus women and children with five loaves and two fish. He then prays alone on the mountain, walks on the sea to his disciples, saves the sinking Peter, and is worshiped as the Son of God. At Gennesaret, all who touch the fringe of his garment are made whole.

Chapter 15

Jesus rebukes the Pharisees and scribes as hypocrites for setting aside God's commandment for their tradition, and teaches that what proceeds from the mouth and heart defiles a person. He heals the Canaanite woman's daughter after testing her with refusals, praising her great faith. He then heals many people by the sea of Galilee, feeds four thousand men plus women and children out of compassion, and sails to the borders of Magdala.

Chapter 16

Jesus rebuked the Pharisees and Sadducees for seeking a sign, warned his disciples about their yeast, and asked who people and the disciples said he was. He accepted Peter's confession that he is the Christ, commanded silence about it, and began foretelling his suffering, death, and resurrection. He also taught about denying oneself and said the Son of Man will come in glory and repay each according to deeds.

Chapter 17

Jesus takes Peter, James, and John up a high mountain, where he is transfigured, his face shining like the sun, and is named by a voice as the beloved Son. He orders silence about the vision until he has risen, heals the boy by rebuking the demon, and predicts his betrayal, death, and third-day raising. In Capernaum he tells Peter to pay the didrachma for both of them with a stater found in a fish's mouth.

Chapter 18

Jesus answers the disciples' question about greatness by setting a little child among them and teaching humility. He warns about stumbling, tells the parable of the lost sheep, instructs on handling a sinning brother, and tells Peter to forgive until seventy times seven. He illustrates this with the parable of the king and the servants, and says he is present where two or three gather in his name.

Chapter 19

Jesus departs from Galilee into Judea beyond the Jordan, heals the multitudes, and answers the Pharisees on divorce by appealing to creation and Moses's concession. He blesses the children, tells the young man to keep the commandments and then to sell his goods and follow him, and teaches that salvation is impossible for men but possible for God. He promises the disciples twelve thrones and a hundredfold reward, ending with 'many will be last who are first.'

Chapter 20

Jesus tells the vineyard parable, foretells his condemnation, crucifixion, and resurrection to the twelve, and answers the request for places in his Kingdom by saying they are not his to give but prepared by his Father. He teaches that greatness means servanthood, and he stops, asks the two blind men what they want, and heals them by touching their eyes out of compassion.

Chapter 21

Jesus enters Jerusalem on a donkey and colt, fulfilling the prophecy of the King who comes humbly. He drives out the temple sellers, heals the lame and blind, curses a fig tree, teaches about faith and prayer, and answers the challenge to his authority with a counter-question and two parables. The chief priests and Pharisees perceive the parables to be about them and seek to seize him but fear the multitudes.

Chapter 22

Jesus tells the parable of the wedding feast and then answers three tests in turn: the Pharisees' tax question, the Sadducees' resurrection puzzle, and a lawyer's question about the greatest commandment. He then asks the Pharisees how the Christ can be David's son and Lord, and no one can answer him or dares ask him more questions.

Chapter 23

Jesus addresses the multitudes and his disciples, instructing them to observe what the scribes and Pharisees teach but not their works, and teaching that one is Father, teacher, and master, and that the greatest is a servant. He pronounces woes on the scribes and Pharisees, calling them hypocrites, blind guides, serpents, and offspring of vipers, and says he will send prophets, wise men, and scribes to them. He laments over Jerusalem, wishing to gather her children like a hen gathers her chicks, and says they will not see him until they say 'Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord!'

Chapter 24

Jesus leaves the temple and predicts that its stones will all be thrown down. On the Mount of Olives he answers the disciples' private question by teaching about warnings, persecution, the great suffering, his coming as Son of Man, and the need to be watchful and ready. He says only the Father knows the day and hour.

Chapter 26

Jesus foretells his crucifixion, defends the woman who anoints him as preparing him for burial, and keeps the Passover with the twelve, predicting his betrayal and Peter's denial. He prays in anguish at Gethsemane, submits to the Father's will, is arrested, rebukes the sword-wielding follower, and before the council says he is the Son of Man who will sit at the right hand of Power. He is spat on, beaten, and declared worthy of death.

Chapter 27

Jesus is bound and delivered to Pilate, answers 'So you say' when asked if he is King of the Jews, and says nothing to the accusations. He is flogged, mocked by soldiers, crucified at Golgotha, cries 'My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?' and yields up his spirit. His body is buried in a new tomb.

Chapter 28

Jesus is announced by the angel as risen from the dead, just as he said. He meets the women, greets them with "Rejoice!", accepts their worship, and tells them to have his brothers go to Galilee. In Galilee he tells the eleven that all authority in heaven and on earth has been given to him, commands them to make disciples of all nations, and promises to be with them always to the end of the age.

Son of Man in The Gospel According to Matthew

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Son of Man first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Son of Man is a figure who will come at an unknown day and hour, so people must watch; he arrives in glory with all the holy angels and sits on his glorious throne.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 25

The Son of Man is said to be coming at a day and hour no one knows, so people are told to watch. He will come in his glory with all the holy angels and sit on the throne of his glory.

Christ in The Gospel According to Matthew

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Christ is a title-bearing figure whose identity is the central question. Peter declares Jesus the Christ, Son of the living God, revealed by the Father, and disciples are told to keep it secret. Later, the Pharisees call the Christ David's son, and Jesus questions that.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 16

Simon Peter declared that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of the living God, which Jesus said was revealed by his Father in heaven rather than by flesh and blood. Jesus then commanded the disciples to tell no one that he was the Christ.

Chapter 22

Jesus asks the Pharisees whose son the Christ is, and they answer that he is David's son. Jesus points out that David in the Spirit calls him Lord and asks how he can then be David's son.

Claims worded by a model

1 claim from 1 quoted fact, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

Christ is son of David (as the Pharisees answered)open the passage →
  1. “What do you think of the Christ? Whose son is he?” They said to him, “Of David.””The Gospel According to Matthew · Chapter 22

Jesus in The Good News According to Mark

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is the central figure of the Gospel of Mark: a Nazarene of Galilee, a carpenter and son of Mary with brothers James, Joses, Judah and Simon and sisters, who is declared beloved Son of God by a voice from the sky, by unclean spirits, and later by a centurion. He is also confessed as the Christ by Peter, and he affirms it himself before the high priest. He calls himself Son of Man and is addressed as Teacher and Lord. The account opens with his coming from Nazareth, baptism by John, and forty days of temptation. He then preaches God's Kingdom in Galilee, with a home in Capernaum. He calls disciples and appoints the twelve, teaching with authority unlike the scribes. He casts out unclean spirits, heals the sick, and forgives sins. He heals the withered hand and claims lordship over the Sabbath. He likens himself to a physician for sinners and to a bridegroom. He teaches in parables, calms a storm, raises Jairus's daughter, feeds multitudes out of compassion, and walks on the sea. He is rejected in his home country, and his teaching, such as that food does not defile, puts him in conflict with Pharisees and scribes. After Peter's confession he repeatedly foretells his suffering, death and rising, and teaches that the Son of Man came to serve and give his life as a ransom. He rides a young donkey into Jerusalem, curses the fig tree, cleanses the temple, and teaches on faith, prayer and forgiveness. He refuses to disclose the source of his authority to his questioners. He says that only the Father knows the day and hour. He is betrayed, arrested, condemned, and handed to Pilate, who asks if he is King of the Jews. He is mocked, crucified between two robbers, and cries out before dying. Women who followed and served him from Galilee are present. He is laid in a tomb, then announced as risen. He appears to Mary Magdalene and to the eleven, commissions them to preach to all creation, goes before the disciples to Galilee as he said, is received up into heaven, and sits at God's right hand.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

16 sections; each heading opens the text

The Good News According to Mark / Chapter 1

Jesus comes from Nazareth of Galilee, is baptized by John, is called beloved Son by a voice from the sky, and is tempted forty days in the wilderness. He then preaches God's Kingdom in Galilee, calls four fishermen, teaches with authority, casts out an unclean spirit, heals Simon's wife's mother, many sick and a leper, and prays in a deserted place, saying he came out to preach in other towns.

Chapter 2

Jesus returns to Capernaum, teaches the crowd, forgives and heals a paralytic, and perceives the scribes' hidden reasoning. He calls Levi, eats with tax collectors and sinners, says he came to call sinners to repentance, and defends his disciples on fasting and the Sabbath. He claims authority on earth to forgive sins and calls the Son of Man lord even of the Sabbath.

Chapter 3

Jesus heals a man's withered hand in the synagogue on the Sabbath after asking whether it is lawful to do good or harm, and he is grieved at the hardness of his opponents' hearts. He draws a great multitude, is named Son of God by unclean spirits whom he sternly silences, and appoints twelve to be with him, preach, and cast out demons. Accused of having Beelzebul and thought insane by his friends, he answers with parables and redefines his family as those who do God's will.

Chapter 4

Jesus teaches the multitude by the sea from a boat, speaking in parables and privately explaining everything to his own disciples. He interprets the parable of the sower, says the mystery of God's Kingdom is given to those around him, and urges them to take heed what they hear. When a wind storm fills the boat, he wakes, rebukes the wind, says to the sea "Peace! Be still!", and brings a great calm, then asks the disciples why they are afraid and lack faith.

Chapter 5

Jesus crosses to the country of the Gadarenes, commands the unclean spirit out of a man, and gives the demons permission to enter the pigs. He is asked by the people to leave, then returns across the sea, heals a woman who touches his clothes, and raises Jairus's daughter, taking only Peter, James, and John with him and strictly ordering that no one should know.

Chapter 6

Jesus teaches in the synagogue in his own country, is rejected by those who know his family, and says a prophet is not without honor except in his own country. He sends out the twelve, has compassion on a great multitude and feeds them, walks on the sea to his disciples, and heals the sick at Gennesaret.

Chapter 7

Jesus rebukes the Pharisees and scribes as hypocrites, saying they set aside God's commandment for human tradition, and teaches that what comes out of a man defiles him, not what goes in. He goes to Tyre and Sidon, tests and then grants a Syrophoenician woman's request, and heals a deaf man with a speech impediment in the Decapolis region, commanding silence that is not kept.

Chapter 8

Jesus feeds four thousand with seven loaves and a few fish, refuses the Pharisees a sign, and warns his disciples about the yeast of the Pharisees and Herod. He heals a blind man at Bethsaida, accepts Peter's confession that he is the Christ, foretells his suffering, death and resurrection, rebukes Peter, and teaches the cost of following him.

Chapter 9

Jesus is transfigured before Peter, James, and John, is declared the beloved Son by a voice from a cloud, and heals a boy by rebuking a mute and deaf spirit. He predicts his death and rising on the third day, and teaches the twelve that the first must be last and servant of all, that those not against them are for them, and that stumbling blocks must be removed.

Chapter 10

Jesus teaches multitudes, answers the Pharisees on divorce, welcomes little children, and tells a man with great possessions to sell all and follow him. He predicts his death and rising, teaches that the Son of Man came to serve and give his life as a ransom for many, and heals Bartimaeus at Jericho.

Chapter 11

Jesus sends disciples for a donkey, rides into Jerusalem to cries of "Hosanna," and enters the temple. He curses a fruitless fig tree, drives out sellers and buyers in the temple, teaches on faith, prayer, and forgiveness, and silences the religious leaders' challenge with a question about John's baptism.

Chapter 12

Jesus speaks in parables, quotes Scripture about the rejected stone, and answers questions about taxes to Caesar, the resurrection, and the greatest commandment. He tells a scribe he is not far from God's Kingdom, asks how the Christ can be David's son, warns against the scribes, and commends the poor widow's gift.

Chapter 13

Jesus predicts the total destruction of the temple's buildings and, on the Mount of Olives, answers Peter, James, John, and Andrew privately about the signs ahead. He warns against deception, foretells persecution, the abomination of desolation, great oppression, and the coming of the Son of Man, and declares that his words will not pass away. He says that of that day or hour no one knows, not even the Son, but only the Father.

Chapter 14

Jesus defends the woman who anoints him, saying she has anointed his body beforehand for burial. He predicts his betrayal, Peter's denial, and the disciples' scattering, prays in anguish at Gethsemane, is arrested, and answers the high priest that he is the Christ, the Son of the Blessed. He is condemned as worthy of death and is spat on and beaten.

Chapter 15

Jesus is bound, handed to Pilate, and answers the question of whether he is King of the Jews with "So you say," then makes no further answer. He is flogged, mocked by the soldiers, crucified at the third hour, and cries "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" before giving up the spirit. His body is taken down, wrapped in linen and laid in a tomb.

Chapter 16

Jesus is announced as risen from the tomb and appears first to Mary Magdalene, then in another form to two on a walk, then to the eleven, whom he rebukes for unbelief. He commissions them to preach the Good News to all creation, then is received up into heaven and sits at the right hand of God.

Christ in The Good News According to Mark

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ is a messianic title and figure. In Chapter 8, Peter identifies Jesus as the Christ, and Jesus commands silence about it. In Chapter 12, Jesus questions how the Christ can be David's son when David, in the Holy Spirit, calls him Lord.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 8

Peter identifies Jesus as the Christ at Caesarea Philippi after the disciples report what others say about him: John the Baptizer, Elijah, or one of the prophets. Jesus commands the disciples to tell no one about him.

Chapter 12

Jesus asks how the scribes can say the Christ is the son of David when David himself, in the Holy Spirit, calls him Lord. He cites the saying 'The Lord said to my Lord, Sit at my right hand.'

Jesus in The Gospel According to Luke

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is a individual presented as the foretold Son of the Most High and Son of God, a Savior, Christ and Lord, who is to receive David's throne and reign over the house of Jacob forever. The records follow his life from announcement to ascension. An angel names him before conception; he is born in Bethlehem as Mary's firstborn, presented at the temple, and at twelve tells the teachers he must be in his Father's house, then grows in wisdom and favor in Nazareth, subject to his parents. At about thirty he is baptized by John, a voice from the sky calls him beloved Son, and the Holy Spirit fills him. He resists the devil's temptations, and demons recognize him as Son of God and Christ. His mission is to preach the good news of God's Kingdom and to seek and save the lost. He teaches with authority in synagogues and the temple, heals lepers and the sick, casts out demons, commands wind and water, raises the dead, forgives sins as Son of Man, calls sinners to repentance, and claims to be lord of the Sabbath. He chooses twelve apostles, names Simon Peter, and later appoints and sends seventy, giving them authority; he says all things were delivered to him by the Father. He is homeless, habitually withdraws to pray, and is accused of gluttony and drunkenness. He sets his face toward Jerusalem, warns of Herod's threat, and foretells his suffering, death and rising. He enters Jerusalem acclaimed as King, teaches daily in the temple, and spends nights on Olivet. Betrayed by Judas Iscariot, one of the twelve, he shares the Passover, is arrested, and before the council affirms he is the Son of God and will sit at God's right hand. Crucified between two criminals under the inscription King of the Jews, he commits his spirit to the Father, dies, and is buried in a tomb. He then rises with flesh and bones, is recognized at Emmaus in the breaking of bread, explains the Scriptures, tells the disciples to await the Father's promise, blesses them at Bethany, and is carried up into heaven.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

23 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 1

Jesus is the son Gabriel says Mary will conceive and name. He is to be great, called the Son of the Most High and Son of God, given the throne of his father David, and reign over the house of Jacob forever in a Kingdom without end.

Chapter 2

Jesus is born in Bethlehem, wrapped in cloth and laid in a feeding trough, and is named Jesus on the eighth day as the angel had given before his conception. He is presented at the temple, recognized by Simeon and Anna, and grows strong in spirit and wisdom. At twelve he stays behind in Jerusalem, amazes the teachers in the temple, says he must be in his Father's house, then returns to Nazareth and is subject to his parents while increasing in wisdom, stature, and favor with God and men.

Chapter 3

Jesus is baptized with the people and, while he prays, the Holy Spirit descends on him like a dove and a voice from the sky calls him beloved Son, in whom God is well pleased. He is about thirty years old when he begins to teach, is supposed to be the son of Joseph, and is traced through a genealogy back to Adam, the son of God.

Chapter 4

Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, is led into the wilderness, fasts forty days, and answers each of the devil's temptations with Scripture. In Nazareth he reads Isaiah, declares it fulfilled, and escapes a crowd that tries to throw him off a cliff. In Capernaum he teaches with authority, casts out an unclean spirit, heals Simon's mother-in-law and many others, forbids demons to speak, and says he was sent to preach God's Kingdom to other cities too.

Chapter 5

Jesus teaches the crowd from Simon's boat, directs the great catch of fish, and tells Simon he will be catching people alive. He cleanses a man full of leprosy, forgives and heals a paralyzed man, calls Levi, and defends eating with sinners and his disciples' not fasting, saying he has come to call sinners to repentance.

Chapter 6

Jesus defends his disciples against the Pharisees by citing David and declares the Son of Man lord of the Sabbath, then heals a man's withered hand on a Sabbath after asking whether it is lawful to do good or harm. He prays all night, chooses twelve apostles, heals the crowd as power comes out of him, and teaches blessings, woes, love of enemies, mercy, and obedience to his words.

Chapter 7

Jesus heals the centurion's servant without entering the house and marvels at the centurion's faith. He raises the widow's son at Nain, answers John's disciples by pointing to his works, and speaks to the crowds about John. At Simon's house he tells the parable of the two debtors, forgives the sinful woman's sins, and tells her that her faith has saved her.

Chapter 8

Jesus preaches through cities and villages, teaches the parable of the sower and explains it to his disciples, and says his true mother and brothers are those who hear and do God's word. He rebukes the wind and water, casts the legion of demons from a man into pigs, heals the woman with the flow of blood, and raises Jairus' daughter by taking her hand and saying 'Child, arise!'

Chapter 9

Jesus sends out the twelve, feeds the multitude, is confessed by Peter as the Christ of God, and is transfigured before Peter, John and James. He twice foretells that he will be delivered up and killed, heals the possessed boy, and sets his face toward Jerusalem, teaching about the cost of following him.

Chapter 10

Jesus appoints and sends out the seventy with instructions, pronounces woes on Chorazin, Bethsaida, and Capernaum, and says he saw Satan fall like lightning from heaven. He rejoices in the Holy Spirit, thanks the Father, and declares that all things have been delivered to him. He answers a lawyer with the parable of the Samaritan, and tells Martha that Mary has chosen the good part.

Chapter 11

Jesus teaches his disciples to pray after they ask, tells the parable of the friend at midnight, and casts out a mute demon. He answers accusations of working by Beelzebul, calls his generation evil, and pronounces woes on the Pharisees and lawyers at a Pharisee's table, after which the scribes and Pharisees lie in wait to accuse him.

Chapter 12

Jesus warns his disciples about hypocrisy, fear, and anxiety, refuses to act as judge over an inheritance dispute, and teaches through a parable of a rich man, images of ravens and lilies, and sayings about watchful servants. He declares that he came to throw fire on the earth, that he has a baptism to undergo and is distressed until it is accomplished, and that he has come to bring division rather than peace. He rebukes the multitudes as hypocrites for failing to interpret the present time.

Chapter 13

Jesus tells his hearers that those killed by Pilate and by the tower were not worse sinners and that all must repent or perish. He heals a bent woman on the Sabbath, rebukes the ruler of the synagogue, teaches parables of the mustard seed and yeast, and urges people to strive for the narrow door. Traveling toward Jerusalem, he answers the Pharisees' warning about Herod by calling him a fox, says he must continue his work, and laments over Jerusalem.

Chapter 14

Jesus, while being watched at a Pharisee ruler's house on a Sabbath, heals a man with dropsy and asks whether healing on the Sabbath is lawful. He teaches guests about the best seats and humility, tells his host to invite those who cannot repay, and speaks the parable of the great supper. He then tells the multitudes that disciples must put him above family and their own life, bear their cross, and renounce all they have.

Chapter 16

Jesus tells his disciples the parable of the dishonest manager and teaches on faithfulness and on serving God versus Mammon. He answers the scoffing Pharisees by saying God knows their hearts, speaks about the law, the Kingdom and divorce, and tells the parable of the rich man and Lazarus.

Chapter 17

Jesus warns the disciples against causing little ones to stumble, instructs them to rebuke and forgive a repentant brother, and teaches about faith and a servant's duty. He cleanses ten lepers by sending them to the priests, commends the returning Samaritan, answers the Pharisees about God's Kingdom, and describes the coming of the Son of Man, who must first suffer and be rejected.

Chapter 18

Jesus tells parables about persistent prayer and about a Pharisee and tax collector, welcomes little children, and tells a rich ruler to sell all and follow him. He predicts to the twelve that he will be delivered to the Gentiles, mocked, scourged, killed, and rise on the third day, then heals a blind man near Jericho, saying his faith has healed him.

Chapter 19

Jesus passes through Jericho, stays at Zacchaeus's house, and says he came to seek and save the lost. He tells the parable of the minas, rides a colt into the Mount of Olives descent to acclaim as King, weeps over Jerusalem and foretells its fall, drives sellers out of the temple, and teaches daily while leaders seek to destroy him.

Chapter 20

Jesus teaches in the temple and silences the priests, scribes and elders by asking about John's baptism, then tells the vineyard parable against them. He answers the question about Caesar's taxes and the Sadducees' resurrection question, asks how the Christ can be David's son, and warns his disciples against the scribes.

Chapter 21

Jesus commends a poor widow's offering and predicts the temple's total destruction when asked about it. He answers his listeners' questions about signs, warning against deceivers, foretelling persecution, the fall of Jerusalem, and the coming of the Son of Man, and urges watchfulness. He teaches daily in the temple and spends nights on the mountain called Olivet.

Chapter 22

Jesus sends Peter and John to prepare the Passover, shares it with the twelve apostles, and gives the bread and cup as his body and the new covenant in his blood. He teaches that the greatest must serve, predicts Peter's denial, prays in agony on the Mount of Olives, heals the servant's ear, and is arrested. Before the council he says the Son of Man will be seated at the right hand of the power of God and affirms that he is the Son of God.

Chapter 23

Jesus is accused before Pilate, answers 'So you say' to the question whether he is King of the Jews, and stays silent before Herod. He is condemned at the crowd's demand, crucified, and prays 'Father, forgive them.' He promises the repentant criminal Paradise, commits his spirit to the Father, and dies; his body is laid in a tomb.

Chapter 24

Jesus joins two disciples on the road to Emmaus without being recognized, explains from Moses and all the prophets the things concerning himself, and is recognized when he breaks bread. He then appears to the gathered disciples in Jerusalem, shows his hands and feet, eats fish and honeycomb, opens their minds to understand the Scriptures, and tells them to wait for the promise of the Father. He blesses them at Bethany and is carried up into heaven.

Christ in The Gospel According to Luke

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Christ is an awaited coming figure, whom people wondered John might be; popularly called David's son, a claim Jesus questions, noting David calls him Lord, leaving the puzzle unresolved.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 20

Jesus asks why people say the Christ is David's son, since David himself calls him Lord in the Psalms. The question of how he can be both is left posed in this unit.

Claims worded by a model

Jesus in The Gospel According to John

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is the central figure of these records from the Gospel According to John: the Word who was with God in the beginning, became flesh, and is full of grace and truth. He is presented as the only born Son of God, sent by the Father, who came down from heaven and is one with the Father. He is also called Lamb of God, King of Israel, Christ, Teacher, Lord, Savior of the world and Son of Man. He is a Jew from Nazareth, called the son of Joseph, with a mother, brothers and disciples. What matters most is his relationship to the Father: he does what pleases the Father, speaks by the Father's commandment, holds authority over judgment and life, and reveals the Father. He describes himself as the bread of life, light of the world, door, good shepherd, resurrection and life, the way, truth and life, and the true vine. The account begins with John's testimony and the calling of disciples, then moves through the wedding at Cana, the cleansing of the temple, and teaching Nicodemus and a Samaritan woman. Signs and conflict follow: healings, feeding the multitude, walking on the sea, and the claim 'before Abraham existed, I AM.' The Jews seek to kill him, and he withdraws and avoids Judea. He gives sight to a blind man, declares he lays down his life and takes it again, and raises Lazarus. The council then resolves to kill him. He enters Jerusalem on a donkey, hailed as King of Israel. With his disciples he washes their feet, gives a new commandment of love, predicts betrayal and Peter's denial, promises the Counselor, says he has overcome the world, and prays for them. In the garden he is seized and bound, questioned, and brought before Pilate, saying his Kingdom is not of this world. He is crucified as King of the Jews, entrusts his mother to the beloved disciple, says 'It is finished,' and is buried. He rises, appears to Mary Magdalene, the disciples and Thomas, gives the Holy Spirit, and at the sea of Tiberias feeds his disciples and commissions Peter to feed his sheep.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

21 sections; each heading opens the text

The Gospel According to John / Chapter 1

Jesus is presented as the Word who was with God and was God, through whom all things were made, and who became flesh full of grace and truth. John identifies him as the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world, the one who baptizes in the Holy Spirit, and the Son of God. He invites two disciples to come and see where he stays, renames Simon as Cephas, calls Philip to follow him, tells Nathanael he saw him under the fig tree, and promises that they will see heaven opened and angels ascending and descending on the Son of Man.

Chapter 2

Jesus is invited with his disciples to the wedding in Cana, where he tells the servants to fill the water pots and the water becomes wine, revealing his glory. He later goes to Capernaum and then Jerusalem, drives the sellers and money changers from the temple, and speaks of destroying and raising up the temple of his body. He does not entrust himself to those who believe in him at the Passover, because he knows everyone and what is in man.

Chapter 3

Jesus is visited by Nicodemus at night and teaches him that one must be born anew, of water and Spirit, to enter God's Kingdom. He speaks of himself as the Son of Man who descended from heaven and will be lifted up, and as the Son God gave so the world might be saved. He then goes into Judea with his disciples, stays there and baptizes, and John's disciples report that everyone is coming to him.

Chapter 4

Jesus leaves Judea for Galilee via Samaria, where, tired, he sits at Jacob's well and speaks with a Samaritan woman, offering her living water, exposing her marital history, and declaring that he is the Messiah. He stays two days with the Samaritans, who call him the Savior of the world, then goes to Galilee, where he heals a nobleman's son at a distance with the words "Your son lives." The text notes that he himself did not baptize, and that this was his second sign after coming out of Judea.

Chapter 5

Jesus goes up to Jerusalem, heals the man at Bethesda on the Sabbath, and later finds him in the temple and tells him to sin no more. He claims that his Father is still working and so is he, that the Son does only what he sees the Father doing, gives life, and has been given all judgment. He cites John, his works, the Father and the Scriptures as testimony about himself, and says Moses wrote about him.

Chapter 6

Jesus feeds the multitude, withdraws when they try to make him king, and walks on the sea to his disciples. At Capernaum he teaches that he is the bread of life who came down from heaven, and that his flesh and blood give eternal life. He knows who does not believe and who will betray him, and he says one of the twelve is a devil.

Chapter 7

Jesus avoids Judea because the Jews seek to kill him, declines his brothers' call to reveal himself, and goes up to the feast in secret. He teaches in the temple that his teaching is from the one who sent him, defends healing on the Sabbath, and on the last day of the feast invites the thirsty to come and drink, promising rivers of living water for those who believe. He says he will be with them a little while longer and that they will seek him and not find him.

Chapter 8

Jesus teaches in the temple, writes on the ground, and challenges the accusers of the woman, then tells her he does not condemn her and to sin no more. He declares himself the light of the world, says he is from above and sent by the Father, and claims that Abraham saw his day and that before Abraham existed, 'I AM.' The Jews take up stones against him, but he hides himself and goes out of the temple.

Chapter 9

Jesus tells his disciples the man's blindness is neither his own nor his parents' sin but a way for God's works to be revealed, and declares himself the light of the world. He makes mud with spit, anoints the man's eyes, and sends him to wash at Siloam, then later finds him after he is expelled and reveals himself as the Son of God, accepting his worship. He says he came for judgment so that those who don't see may see and those who see may become blind, and tells the Pharisees their sin remains.

Chapter 10

Jesus tells the sheep fold parable, then identifies himself as the door and the good shepherd who lays down his life and takes it again by the Father's commandment. At the Feast of the Dedication he says his sheep hear him, that he and the Father are one, and defends himself against the charge of blasphemy by appealing to Scripture and his works. He escapes seizure and goes beyond the Jordan, where many believe in him.

Chapter 11

Jesus delays two days after hearing that Lazarus is sick, then goes to Judea and tells his disciples plainly that Lazarus is dead. He declares himself the resurrection and the life, weeps at the tomb, prays to the Father, and calls Lazarus out. Because the council resolves to kill him, he withdraws to Ephraim with his disciples, and the authorities order that anyone who knows where he is must report it.

Chapter 12

Jesus comes to Bethany six days before the Passover, defends Mary's anointing as preparation for his burial, and enters Jerusalem sitting on a young donkey as the crowd hails him as King of Israel. He announces that the time has come for the Son of Man to be glorified, says that when lifted up from the earth he will draw all people to himself, and then hides from the crowd. He declares that he came as a light to save rather than judge the world and speaks only what the Father commanded.

Chapter 13

Jesus, knowing his time had come to depart to the Father, lays aside his outer garments, washes the disciples' feet, and tells them to do likewise as an example. He announces that one of them will betray him, identifies Judas by giving him dipped bread, and tells him to do quickly what he does. He then speaks of glorification, gives a new commandment of love, and predicts that Peter will deny him three times.

Chapter 14

Jesus comforts his disciples, saying he is going to prepare a place for them and will come again to receive them. He declares himself the way, the truth, and the life, says whoever has seen him has seen the Father, and promises that the Father will send the Counselor. He gives his peace, states that the Father is greater than he, and ends by telling the disciples to arise and leave.

Chapter 15

Jesus calls himself the true vine and says apart from him his followers can do nothing. He commands them to remain in his love and love one another, says he has loved them as the Father loved him, and calls them friends, having chosen and appointed them. He tells them the world will hate them as it hated him, and says he will send the Counselor from the Father.

Chapter 16

Jesus warns his disciples of coming persecution, says it is to their advantage that he goes away so he can send the Counselor, and speaks of going to the Father. He tells them that they will be scattered and leave him alone, yet he is not alone because the Father is with him, and he declares that he has overcome the world.

Chapter 17

Jesus prays to the Father, asking to be glorified with the glory he had before the world existed, and says he has glorified the Father on earth and accomplished the work given him. He reports that he revealed the Father's name, gave the disciples the Father's words, and kept them, losing only the son of destruction. He sanctifies himself for the disciples' sake and prays for them and for future believers.

Chapter 18

Jesus goes to the garden, identifies himself to those seeking him, and asks that his disciples be allowed to go. He rebukes Peter for using the sword, is bound, questioned by the high priest, slapped by an officer, and sent to Caiaphas and then Pilate. Before Pilate he says his Kingdom is not of this world and that he came to testify to the truth.

Chapter 19

Jesus is flogged, mocked with a crown of thorns and purple garment, and brought out by Pilate. He tells Pilate that Pilate would have no power unless it were given from above, and that the one who delivered him has greater sin. He carries his cross to Golgotha and is crucified between two others. He entrusts his mother to the disciple he loved, says "I am thirsty," receives vinegar, says "It is finished," and gives up his spirit. His side is pierced after death, and his body is buried in a new tomb.

Chapter 20

Jesus appears to Mary Magdalene at the tomb, calls her by name, and tells her not to hold him since he has not yet ascended to his Father, sending her to his brothers. That evening he stands among the locked-in disciples, says 'Peace be to you,' shows his hands and side, sends them as the Father sent him, and breathes on them to give the Holy Spirit. After eight days he appears again and invites Thomas to touch his hands and side, and blesses those who believe without seeing.

Chapter 21

Jesus stands on the beach at daybreak, unrecognized, and tells the disciples to cast the net on the right side, leading to a huge catch. He serves them bread and fish, asks Peter three times about his love, commissions him to feed his sheep, and tells him to follow. He also tells Peter that the beloved disciple's fate is none of his concern.

Jesus in The Acts of the Apostles

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is the central figure of these records: a man from Nazareth, son of Mary with brothers, and Son of God whose Father is God. He was approved by God through mighty works, wonders and signs, and anointed with the Holy Spirit and power. He went about doing good and healing those oppressed by the devil. He is called Servant of God, Holy and Righteous One, Prince of life, Prince and Savior, Christ, and Lord of all. The records open with him chosen apostles, giving them commandment through the Holy Spirit, and showing himself alive to them by many proofs over forty days after he suffered. He told them to wait for the Father's promise, said they would be witnesses, and was taken up in a cloud, with a promise that he would return the same way. The early chapters recount that lawless men, the Jerusalem people and rulers, crucified or killed him by hanging on a tree, by God's determined counsel, though no cause for death was found. God raised him from the dead without decay, exalted him to his right hand, and made him Lord and Christ. Heaven receives him until the times of restoration of all things. He is the rejected stone that became the head of the corner, and there is no other name under heaven by which people must be saved. Faith in his name heals the lame man, and the apostles keep preaching and teaching him despite the council's command and suffering. Stephen sees him standing at God's right hand and calls on him as Lord. He is appointed Judge of the living and the dead, and everyone who believes in his name receives forgiveness of sins. The story then spreads outward: Philip preaches to the Samaritans and the eunuch, Gentiles receive the same gift, and believers preach the Lord Jesus in Antioch. Jesus himself speaks to Saul from a light, identifies himself as the one Saul persecuted, and appoints him servant and witness to Jews and Gentiles, to bear his name before nations and kings. Paul then proclaims in synagogues and cities that Jesus is the Christ who had to suffer and rise, a Saviour from David's offspring, and that God will judge the world by him. The records end in Rome with Paul teaching about the Lord Jesus Christ with all boldness.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

17 sections; each heading opens the text

The Acts of the Apostles / Chapter 1

Jesus showed himself alive to the apostles after he suffered, by many proofs over forty days, speaking about God's Kingdom. He told them to wait in Jerusalem for the Father's promise, said they would receive power and be witnesses, and was taken up as a cloud received him out of sight. Two men in white said he would come back in the same way he went.

Chapter 2

Jesus is described as a man approved by God through mighty works, wonders, and signs, delivered up by God's counsel and crucified by lawless men. God raised him from death, exalted him to his right hand, and made him both Lord and Christ; baptism is to be in his name.

Chapter 3

His name is the one in which Peter commands the lame man to walk, and Peter says faith in his name made the man strong. Peter calls him God's glorified Servant, the Holy and Righteous One and Prince of life, whom the people delivered up and denied before Pilate and killed, but whom God raised from the dead. Heaven must receive him until the times of restoration, and God sent him first to the people to bless them.

Chapter 4

Jesus is the name in which the crippled man was healed, and Peter says he was crucified by the rulers but raised by God. He is called the stone rejected by the builders that became the head of the corner, and the only name under heaven by which people must be saved. The believers' prayer calls him God's holy Servant, whom God anointed and against whom Herod, Pontius Pilate, the Gentiles, and the people of Israel gathered.

Chapter 5

Peter says that God raised up Jesus, whom the council killed by hanging him on a tree, and exalted him as Prince and Savior to give repentance and remission of sins. The council commanded the apostles not to speak in his name, but the apostles rejoiced to suffer dishonor for it and kept teaching and preaching him as the Christ.

Chapter 7

Jesus is seen by Stephen standing at the right hand of God, whom Stephen calls the Son of Man. Stephen calls on him as Lord, asking him to receive his spirit. He is also referred to as the Righteous One whom the council has betrayed and murdered.

Chapter 8

Philip proclaimed Jesus Christ to the Samaritans, along with good news about God's Kingdom and his name. The Samaritans had been baptized in his name, and Philip preached about Jesus to the eunuch starting from the Isaiah passage.

Chapter 9

Jesus spoke from the light to Saul, identifying himself as the one Saul was persecuting and telling him to enter the city. He told Ananias in a vision that Saul was his chosen vessel to bear his name to the nations, kings and Israel, and that Saul would suffer for his name. Peter healed Aeneas and raised Tabitha in his name.

Chapter 10

Peter describes Jesus Christ as Lord of all, who brought good news of peace, was from Nazareth, and was anointed by God with the Holy Spirit and power. He went about doing good and healing those oppressed by the devil, was killed by hanging on a tree, and was raised by God on the third day and shown to chosen witnesses who ate and drank with him. He is appointed Judge of the living and the dead, all the prophets testify that everyone who believes in his name receives remission of sins, and the new believers are baptized in his name.

Chapter 11

Jesus Christ is the Lord in whom Peter and the others believed, and the Gentiles received the same gift when they believed. Believers from Cyprus and Cyrene preach the Lord Jesus to the Hellenists in Antioch.

Chapter 13

Paul preaches that Jesus came from David's offspring as the promised savior, announced by John, and was condemned by the Jerusalem rulers though no cause for death was found. God raised him from the dead without decay, and through him forgiveness of sins and justification are proclaimed.

Chapter 17

Paul proclaimed that Jesus is the Christ who had to suffer and rise from the dead. In Thessalonica his accusers charged that Jesus was another king contrary to Caesar's decrees. In Athens, Paul's preaching of Jesus and the resurrection led some to think he advocated foreign deities, and he said God will judge the world by a man he ordained and raised from the dead.

Chapter 18

Paul testifies to the Jews at Corinth that Jesus is the Christ. Apollos teaches accurately about Jesus, though knowing only John's baptism, and later shows publicly from the Scriptures that Jesus is the Christ.

Chapter 19

Paul says John told people to believe in the one who would come after him, Christ Jesus. The disciples are baptized in his name, and the exorcists try to invoke him, but the evil spirit says it knows Jesus. After the exorcists' defeat the name of the Lord Jesus is magnified in Ephesus.

Chapter 22

Jesus of Nazareth identifies himself to Saul in a great light near Damascus, asking why Saul persecutes him and directing him into the city. Later he appears to Paul in a temple trance, telling him to leave Jerusalem because they will not receive his testimony and that he will send him far away to the Gentiles.

Chapter 26

Paul says he once believed he should act against the name of Jesus of Nazareth. Paul reports that Jesus spoke to him from the light on the road, identified himself as the one Paul was persecuting, and appointed him a servant and witness to the Jews and Gentiles.

Chapter 28

Paul persuades people concerning Jesus from the law of Moses and the prophets at his lodging in Rome. At the end Paul is said to be teaching the things concerning the Lord Jesus Christ with all boldness.

Jesus Christ in Paul’s Letter to the Romans

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ, called 'our Lord', is presented in these records as the Son of God, descended from David and Israel according to the flesh, and declared Son of God with power by the resurrection. He is also described as over all, God, blessed forever. The records open with his identity and Paul's grace and apostleship received through him. They then turn to his saving work. God sent him as an atoning sacrifice, and righteousness comes by faith in him. He was delivered up for trespasses and raised for justification. He died for the ungodly, sinners and enemies, at the right time. Through him believers have peace with God, access into grace, and reconciliation. He is set against Adam as the one man whose obedience brings justification to life. Believers are baptized into his death. He was raised through the Father's glory, death no longer has dominion over him, and he lives to God. In him there is no condemnation. Believers die to the law through his body and are joined to the risen one. He sits at God's right hand interceding for believers, is firstborn among many brothers, and believers are joint heirs with him. He is the fulfillment of the law for righteousness to all who believe, and confessing Jesus as Lord is part of being saved. He is the stumbling stone laid in Zion, yet those who believe in him will not be disappointed. In him the many are one body. He is Lord of both the dead and the living, all will stand before his judgment seat, and a fellow believer is one for whom he died. Readers are exhorted to put on the Lord Jesus Christ. Finally, he did not please himself, accepted believers to the glory of God, and became a servant of the circumcision to confirm the promises to the fathers so the Gentiles might glorify God. Paul boasts in his work through him for Gentile obedience. The records close with greetings from the assemblies of Christ and an invocation of his grace.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

13 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is described as God's Son, born of David's offspring according to the flesh and declared Son of God with power by the resurrection from the dead. He is called 'our Lord', and through him Paul received grace and apostleship; grace and peace come from God the Father and from him.

Chapter 3

Jesus Christ is the one through whom the righteousness of God comes by faith to all who believe. God sent him as an atoning sacrifice through faith in his blood, and the redemption that is in him is the means by which people are justified freely by grace.

Chapter 5

Jesus Christ is the one through whom believers have peace with God, access into grace, and reconciliation. He died for the ungodly while they were sinners and enemies, and believers will be saved by his life. As the one man whose obedience and act of righteousness bring abundant grace, justification to life, and many made righteous, he is set against Adam, who foreshadows him.

Chapter 6

Christ Jesus is the one into whom believers were baptized, and they were baptized into his death. He was raised from the dead through the Father's glory, dies no more, and death no longer has dominion over him. He lives to God, and eternal life is given in him.

Chapter 7

Through the body of Christ, believers were made dead to the law so they could be joined to him who was raised from the dead and bear fruit to God. He is also named as the one through whom the narrator thanks God, being called Jesus Christ, our Lord.

Chapter 8

Jesus Christ is the one in whom there is no condemnation for believers, and God sent him as his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh. He was raised from the dead by God, is at the right hand of God, and makes intercession for believers. He is the firstborn among many brothers, and believers are joint heirs with him.

Chapter 9

Christ is named as the one for whose sake Paul would wish himself accursed, and Paul speaks the truth in Christ. He came from the Israelites according to the flesh and is described as over all, God, blessed forever. The stumbling stone and rock of offense laid in Zion is tied to belief in him, and those who believe in him will not be disappointed.

Chapter 10

Christ is the fulfillment of the law for righteousness to everyone who believes. No one needs to ascend to heaven to bring him down or descend into the abyss to bring him up from the dead, and God raised him from the dead. Confessing Jesus as Lord is part of being saved.

Chapter 12

Christ is the one in whom the many believers are one body. Individually they are members of one another in him.

Chapter 13

In the final verse, the readers are exhorted to put on the Lord Jesus Christ as the alternative to making provision for the flesh and its lusts.

Chapter 14

Christ died, rose, and lived again so that he might be Lord of both the dead and the living. All will stand before his judgment seat, and he is the one for whom a brother is said to have died. Paul is persuaded in the Lord Jesus that nothing is unclean of itself, and glory is ascribed to God through Jesus Christ.

Chapter 15

Christ did not please himself, and he accepted the believers to the glory of God. He was made a servant of the circumcision to confirm the promises to the fathers and so that the Gentiles might glorify God for his mercy. Paul boasts in him and says Christ worked through Paul by word and deed for the obedience of the Gentiles.

Chapter 16

Jesus Christ is named as the one in whom Paul's fellow workers labor, and the one whom the divisive people fail to serve. The assemblies of Christ send greetings. His grace is invoked on the recipients at the close of the chapter.

Jesus Christ in Paul’s First Letter to the Corinthians

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ, as presented across Paul's First Letter to the Corinthians, is the Son of God and Lord, the central figure of the believers' faith. The letter opens by calling him Lord and Son of God, into whose fellowship the Corinthians were called; he is the power and wisdom of God, made to believers wisdom, righteousness, sanctification and redemption. Paul determines to know only him crucified, the Lord of glory whom the rulers of this world crucified in ignorance. He is the one foundation laid, the believers are his, and he belongs to God. Apostles are his servants, and Paul became the Corinthians' father in Christ Jesus. He is called our Passover, sacrificed in our place, and the one Lord through whom are all things. He died for the weak brother, so wounding believers' consciences is sinning against him. He is identified as the spiritual rock that followed the fathers in the wilderness. The Lord's table shares his blood and body, and on the night he was betrayed he gave the words of the new covenant in his blood. He is head of every man, with God as his head, and the church is his body with many members. The fullest account comes in chapter 15: he died for sins according to the Scriptures, was buried, was raised on the third day, and appeared to Cephas, the twelve, over five hundred brothers, James, all the apostles, and last to Paul. He is the first fruits of those asleep, the second man from heaven and the last Adam, a life-giving spirit, who gives life to all in Christ. He must reign until all enemies are under his feet, then deliver the Kingdom to God the Father and himself be subjected to God. The letter closes with the prayer 'Come, Lord!', a curse on anyone who does not love him, and a wish for his grace and Paul's love in Christ Jesus to be with the Corinthians.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

11 sections; each heading opens the text

Paul’s First Letter to the Corinthians / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is called Lord and Son of God, and the Corinthians were called into fellowship with him and will be confirmed blameless in his day. Paul asks whether Christ is divided, preaches him as crucified, and describes him as the power and wisdom of God, made to believers wisdom, righteousness, sanctification, and redemption.

Chapter 2

Jesus Christ, crucified, is the one thing the speaker determined to know among the brothers. He is called the Lord of glory, whom the rulers of this world crucified because they did not know God's wisdom. Believers are said to have his mind.

Chapter 3

Jesus Christ is identified as the one foundation that has been laid, and no one can lay any other. The believers are Christ's, and Christ is God's.

Chapter 4

Christ is the one whose servants the apostles are, and for whose sake they are fools. Paul became the Corinthians' father in Christ Jesus through the Good News, and Timothy will remind them of Paul's ways which are in Christ.

Chapter 5

Jesus Christ is named as the Lord in whose name and with whose power the believers are to gather and deliver the offender to Satan. He is called "our Passover," who has been sacrificed in our place, and the day of the Lord Jesus is when the man's spirit may be saved.

Chapter 8

Jesus Christ is named as the one Lord, through whom are all things and through whom believers live. Christ died for the weak brother, and sinning against the brothers by wounding their conscience is sinning against him.

Chapter 10

Christ is identified as the spiritual rock that followed the fathers in the wilderness, and some of them tested him and perished by serpents. The cup and bread of the Lord's table are described as a sharing of his blood and body.

Chapter 11

Christ is the model Paul imitates, and is described as the head of every man, with God as his head. The text also recounts that the Lord Jesus, on the night he was betrayed, took bread and the cup and gave the words of the new covenant in his blood.

Chapter 12

Christ is likened to a body that is one yet has many members. The believers addressed are said to be the body of Christ, and members individually.

Chapter 15

Christ died for sins according to the Scriptures, was buried, was raised on the third day, and appeared to Cephas, the twelve, over five hundred brothers, James, all the apostles, and the speaker. He is the first fruits of those asleep, will reign until all enemies are under his feet, and will then deliver up the Kingdom to God the Father and himself be subjected to God. He is called the last Adam, a life-giving spirit, and the second man, the Lord from heaven.

Chapter 16

Referred to as the Lord Jesus Christ, he is the one whom anyone who does not love is to be cursed. The text includes the prayer 'Come, Lord!' and asks that his grace be with the Corinthians, with Paul's love extended to them in Christ Jesus.
