Christ in 2 Corinthians

Paul · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ, called Jesus Christ, is presented across Paul's Second Letter to the Corinthians as the Son of God, the image of God, and the Lord preached by the apostles. The letter opens with him as the "Yes" to all God's promises, the one through whom comes the "Amen" to God's glory, and the source of comfort that abounds as his sufferings abound to Paul and his companions. Through the middle chapters he is the one in whose presence Paul forgives, in whom God leads the ministers in triumph, and whose letter the Corinthian believers are shown to be. In him the veil over the old covenant passes away. The letter then centers on his death and resurrection: he died for all so that the living might live for him, he knew no sin yet was made sin on our behalf, and God was in him reconciling the world. He holds the judgment seat before which all must be revealed. His putting to death is carried in the ministers' bodies so that his life may be revealed. He has no agreement with Belial, which grounds the call not to be yoked with unbelievers. He is the model of grace, becoming poor though rich for the Corinthians' sakes. His humility and gentleness ground Paul's entreaty, and every thought is to be taken captive to obedience to him. Paul promises the Corinthians to him as one husband and warns against false apostles who masquerade as his. The portrait closes with him crucified through weakness yet living through God's power, present in the believers unless they are disqualified, and with the benediction invoking his grace.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

10 sections; each heading opens the text

Paul’s Second Letter to the Corinthians / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is the Lord and the Son of God, preached among the Corinthians by Paul, Silvanus, and Timothy. The sufferings of Christ abound to Paul's group, and comfort abounds through Christ. In him all God's promises are "Yes," and through him comes the "Amen" to God's glory.

Chapter 2

Christ is the one in whose presence Paul forgives for the recipients' sakes, and the Good News of Christ is what brought Paul to Troas. God leads Paul and his companions in triumph in Christ, they are a sweet aroma of Christ to God, and they speak in Christ.

Chapter 3

Christ is the one whose letter the Corinthian believers are shown to be, a letter served by Paul and his co-workers. Through Christ they have confidence toward God. In Christ the veil over the reading of the old covenant passes away.

Chapter 4

Jesus Christ is described as the image of God, whose glory is the subject of the Good News, and he is preached as Lord. His putting to death is carried in the ministers' bodies so that his life may be revealed in their mortal flesh. God raised him, and through him God will raise the ministers.

Chapter 5

Christ's love compels the ministers, who judge that he died for all, so that those who live should live for him who died and rose again. God was in Christ reconciling the world to himself. He knew no sin but was made to be sin on our behalf, and he holds the judgment seat before which all must be revealed.

Chapter 6

Christ appears in a rhetorical question: what agreement does Christ have with Belial? This is used to show that believers should not be yoked with unbelievers.

Chapter 8

Jesus Christ is cited as the model of grace: though he was rich, for the addressees' sakes he became poor, so that through his poverty they might become rich.

Chapter 10

Christ's humility and gentleness are the basis of Paul's entreaty, and every thought is to be brought into captivity to the obedience of Christ. Paul says that anyone who trusts he belongs to Christ should consider that Paul also belongs to Christ. The Good News of Christ is what Paul brought as far as Corinth.

Chapter 11

Christ is the one husband to whom Paul promised the Corinthians, so that he might present them as a pure virgin. Paul speaks of the simplicity that is in Christ and of the truth of Christ being in him, and he warns against false apostles who masquerade as Christ's apostles.

Chapter 13

Jesus Christ is said to speak in Paul and to be powerful in the Corinthians. He was crucified through weakness yet lives through the power of God. Paul says Christ is in the believers unless they are disqualified, and the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ is invoked on them in the closing benediction.

Jesus Christ in Paul’s Letter to the Galatians

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ, called Lord, is the central figure of Paul's Letter to the Galatians. He was raised from the dead by God the Father and gave himself for sins to deliver people from this present evil age, according to God's will. Paul received the Good News through a revelation of him and became an apostle through him. Paul says Christ lives in him and loves him. Christ is the singular offspring of Abraham's promise. He redeemed believers from the law's curse by becoming a curse for them, and he makes them free. Righteousness through the law would make his death pointless; those seeking justification by the law are alienated from him, and circumcision profits nothing. The letter shows him crucified among the Galatians, then closes with his cross as the sole ground of boasting, his marks borne on the author's body, and a prayer for his grace to be with the brothers' spirit.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Paul’s Letter to the Galatians / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is named as the one through whom Paul is made an apostle, and he was raised from the dead by God the Father. He is called Lord, gave himself for sins to deliver people out of this present evil age according to God's will, and a revelation of him is how Paul received the Good News.

Chapter 2

Jesus Christ is the one in whom believers seek to be justified, and Paul denies that this makes Christ a servant of sin. Paul says Christ lives in him, and that the Son of God loved him and gave himself up for him. If righteousness came through the law, Christ would have died for nothing.

Chapter 3

Jesus Christ was openly portrayed among the Galatians as crucified. He redeemed believers from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for them, and he is identified as the singular 'offspring' to whom the promise to Abraham was made.

Chapter 5

Christ is said to have made believers free, and he will profit nothing those who receive circumcision. Those who seek justification by the law are alienated from him, and those who belong to him have crucified the flesh with its passions and lusts.

Chapter 6

Jesus Christ is called Lord, and keeping his law is described as bearing one another's burdens. His cross is the sole ground of the author's boasting, and the author bears his marks on his body. The letter closes with a prayer for his grace to be with the brothers' spirit.

Jesus Christ in Paul’s Letter to the Ephesians

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ, as presented across the six chapters of Paul's Letter to the Ephesians, is the exalted Lord and head of the assembly (the church), his body. The records open with his supremacy: God raised him from the dead, seated him at his right hand in the heavenly places, set him far above every rule, authority, power and dominion, put all things under his feet, and made him head over all things for the assembly. In him believers are chosen, redeemed through his blood, forgiven, and given an inheritance, and all things are to be summed up in him. Next comes his reconciling work: he is our peace, makes Jews and Gentiles one, breaks down the dividing wall, abolishes the law of commandments in his flesh, and reconciles both to God through the cross, giving both access to the Father in one Spirit. He is the chief cornerstone of the foundation of the apostles and prophets. Through him God created all things and accomplished his eternal purpose; Paul prays that he may dwell in hearts and that readers may know his love. He then ascends far above the heavens, fills all things, and gives apostles, prophets, evangelists, shepherds and teachers as gifts, while the body grows into him as head. Finally he is the model of love: he loved the assembly and gave himself up as an offering and sacrifice to God, as savior of the body, sanctifying her by the washing of water with the word to present her holy and without defect. The letter closes by calling him Lord, whom servants serve, and a source with God the Father of peace, love and faith, with grace for all who love him.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Paul’s Letter to the Ephesians / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is the one in whom believers are chosen, redeemed through his blood, forgiven, and given an inheritance, and in whom all things in heaven and on earth are to be summed up. God raised him from the dead, seated him at his right hand far above all rule and authority, put all things under his feet, and made him head over all things for the assembly, his body.

Chapter 2

Christ Jesus is the one with whom God made believers alive, raised them up and seated them in the heavenly places. He is called our peace: he made both one, broke down the middle wall, abolished the hostility in his flesh, and reconciled both to God through the cross. He preached peace to those far and near, and is the chief cornerstone of the building.

Chapter 3

Jesus Christ is the one whose prisoner Paul is, and the one through whom God created all things. God accomplished his eternal purpose in him, and in him believers have boldness and access in confidence through faith. Paul prays that Christ may dwell in the readers' hearts through faith and that they may know his love, which surpasses knowledge; glory is given to God in Christ Jesus to all generations.

Chapter 4

Christ ascended on high, leading captivity captive and giving gifts to people, having first descended into the lower parts of the earth, and he fills all things. He is the head into whom the body grows, the measure of fullness the believers are to reach, and the one in whom God forgave them.

Chapter 5

Christ is presented as the model of love, having loved the readers and given himself up for them as an offering and sacrifice to God. He is head of the assembly and savior of the body, and he sanctified the assembly by cleansing her with the washing of water with the word so he could present her gloriously holy and without defect. He will shine on those who awake from sleep and arise from the dead, and the passage's mystery of one flesh is said to concern him and the assembly.

Chapter 6

The Lord Jesus Christ is the one whom servants serve and obey as to Christ, and the source, together with God the Father, of peace and love with faith. Grace is wished upon all who love him with incorruptible love.

Christ in Paul’s Letter to the Colossians

Paul · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ, called Jesus Christ and the Son of God's love, is presented as supreme over creation and the church. Chapter 1 describes him as the image of the invisible God, firstborn of all creation and from the dead, through whom and for whom all things were created and are held together. He is head of the assembly, and through his cross's blood he redeems and reconciles all things. Chapter 2 stresses that all the fullness of the Deity dwells in him bodily and all wisdom and knowledge are hidden in him. He is head of principalities and powers, over which he triumphed, and the body grows from him as Head. Readers are to walk in him and be made full in him. Chapter 3 places him seated at God's right hand as the readers' life, to be revealed in glory with them. He forgave them, modeling how they should forgive one another, and he is all and in all, erasing distinctions among people. They are to do everything in his name and serve him as Lord.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is described as the Son of God's love, in whom believers have redemption and forgiveness of sins. He is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of all creation, before all things, who holds all things together, and the head of the body, the assembly, and the firstborn from the dead. All things were created through him and for him, and through the blood of his cross he made peace and reconciles all things to himself.

Chapter 2

Christ is the one in whom all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hidden and in whom all the fullness of the Deity dwells bodily. He is the head of all principality and power, and he triumphed over the principalities and powers. The recipients are to walk in him, are made full in him, and are told to hold firmly to him as the Head, from whom the whole body grows.

Chapter 3

Christ is described as seated on the right hand of God and as the readers' life, who will be revealed in glory with them. He forgave the readers, serving as the model for their forgiveness of each other, and he is all and in all. The readers are to do everything in his name and serve him as Lord.

Lord Jesus Christ in 1 Thessalonians

Paul · the work’s page · read it where Lord Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Lord Jesus Christ is God's Son, named with God the Father as the one in whom the assembly exists and a source of grace and peace. Certain Jews killed him, yet God raised him from the dead; he died and rose again, which grounds believers' hope, and he died for them so they may live together with him whether they wake or sleep. The Thessalonians imitated him and await him from heaven, and he delivers them from the wrath to come. He is also an avenger in matters of sexual immorality and wronging others. The records look ahead to his coming: he will descend from heaven with a shout, the archangel's voice and God's trumpet, come with all his saints, and be with believers forever. At that coming their hearts, spirit, soul and body are to be kept blameless in holiness. The letter closes with a blessing of his grace.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

1 Thessalonians / Chapter 1

The Lord Jesus Christ is named with God the Father as the one in whom the assembly exists and as a source of grace and peace. The Thessalonians' hope is in him, and they imitated him. He is God's Son, whom God raised from the dead, whom the Thessalonians await from heaven, and who delivers us from the wrath to come.

Chapter 2

He is called the Lord Jesus, whom certain Jews killed. The speakers are described as apostles of Christ, and the believers will be before him at his coming.

Chapter 3

Jesus is called Lord and is asked, together with God the Father, to direct the authors' way to the Thessalonians. His coming is anticipated, when he will come with all his saints and the believers' hearts are to be established blameless in holiness. The Good News is identified as the Good News of Christ.

Chapter 4

The Lord Jesus is the one through whom the writers gave instructions, and the one in whom they exhort the believers. He is described as an avenger in matters of sexual immorality and wronging others, and as one who died and rose again. He will himself descend from heaven with a shout, the archangel's voice and God's trumpet, and believers will be with him forever.

Chapter 5

He is the one through whom God appointed believers to obtain salvation, and he died for them so that they might live together with him whether they wake or sleep. His coming is the point at which the recipients' whole spirit, soul, and body are to be preserved blameless, and the letter closes with a blessing of his grace.

Christ Jesus in 1 Timothy

Paul · the work’s page · read it where Christ Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ Jesus is presented in 1 Timothy as both a man and Lord, the hope of Paul and Timothy, and the source of grace, mercy, and peace alongside God. He came into the world to save sinners, enabling and appointing Paul and showing patience as an example for believers. He is the only mediator between God and men, who gave himself as a ransom for all. He made the good confession before Pontius Pilate. His appearing is awaited at the right time, when he will be shown as the blessed and only Ruler, King of kings and Lord of lords, who alone has immortality and dwells in unapproachable light.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

1 Timothy / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is called our hope and Lord, and is named alongside God as the source of grace, mercy, and peace. He enabled Paul, counted him faithful, and appointed him to service. The text says he came into the world to save sinners and displayed all his patience in Paul as an example to those who would believe in him for eternal life.

Chapter 2

Christ Jesus is identified as the one mediator between God and men, and as a man. He gave himself as a ransom for all, which is described as the testimony at the proper time.

Chapter 6

Jesus Christ is named as the source of the sound words to which true doctrine is tied. He testified the good confession before Pontius Pilate, and his appearing is the point until which the commandment is to be kept blameless. At the right time he will be shown as the blessed and only Ruler, King of kings and Lord of lords.

Jesus Christ in 2 Timothy

Paul · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ is presented as Savior and Lord, descended from David and risen from the dead. In chapter 1, he appears as Savior, abolishes death, and brings life and immortality to light through the Good News, with grace given in him before eternal times. In chapter 2, he is the source of strengthening grace, the one believers die, live, and reign with, and he remains faithful even when people are faithless.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

2 Timothy / Chapter 1

Christ Jesus is the one in whom the promise of life lies and from whom, with God the Father, grace, mercy, and peace come. As Savior he appeared, abolished death, and brought life and immortality to light through the Good News; grace was given in him before times eternal.

Chapter 2

Jesus Christ is presented as the source of grace in which the addressee is to be strengthened, and the one to be remembered as risen from the dead and of the offspring of David. He is the one with whom believers die, live, and reign, and he remains faithful even when people are faithless, for he can't deny himself.

Jesus in The Letter to the Hebrews

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus, called the Son, is presented across chapters 1-4, 6 and 7 of the Letter to the Hebrews as superior to angels and Moses and as the perfected, eternal high priest. He is appointed heir of all things, the radiance of God's glory and image of his substance, who upholds all things by the word of his power. He laid the earth's foundation, made the heavens, and remains the same forever while they perish. He is addressed as God with an everlasting throne and scepter of uprightness, loves righteousness and hates iniquity, and is anointed above his fellows. After purifying sins he sits at the right hand of the Majesty on high, awaiting the time his enemies become his footstool. The account then turns to his humanity. He was made a little lower than the angels, shared flesh and blood, and tasted death for everyone. Through death he brings the devil to nothing, and he is crowned with glory and honor. Made perfect through sufferings, he is the author of salvation, calls the offspring of Abraham brothers without shame, and can help the tempted because he was tempted himself. He is a merciful, faithful, sympathetic and sinless high priest who has passed through the heavens. He is called Apostle and High Priest of the readers' confession, faithful as a Son over God's house, and worthy of more glory than Moses. The final movement concerns his priesthood. He entered within the veil as a forerunner and is a priest forever after the order of Melchizedek, though he descends from Judah, a tribe about which Moses said nothing concerning priesthood. He was made priest with an oath sworn by God and is guarantee of a better covenant. Because he lives forever, his priesthood is unchangeable, and he is able to save to the uttermost those who draw near to God through him. He is holy, guiltless, undefiled, separated from sinners and higher than the heavens, the Son perfected forever.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

The Letter to the Hebrews / Chapter 1

The Son is the one through whom God has spoken at the end of these days, the appointed heir of all things, and the one through whom the worlds were made. He is the radiance of God's glory and the image of his substance, upholds all things by the word of his power, purified us of our sins, and sat down at the right hand of the Majesty on high. He has inherited a name more excellent than the angels', is addressed as God with an eternal throne, is said to have loved righteousness and hated iniquity, is addressed as the Lord who laid the earth's foundation and who remains the same, and is invited to sit at God's right hand until his enemies are made his footstool.

Chapter 2

Jesus is the one made a little lower than the angels and crowned with glory and honor because of the suffering of death, tasting death for everyone by God's grace. He is the author of salvation made perfect through sufferings, is not ashamed to call those he sanctifies brothers, and shared flesh and blood to bring the devil to nothing. He was made like his brothers to become a merciful and faithful high priest who makes atonement for the people's sins, and having suffered being tempted, he can help those who are tempted.

Chapter 3

Jesus is called the Apostle and High Priest of the readers' confession and is to be considered by them. He was faithful to God who appointed him and is counted worthy of more glory than Moses, being faithful as a Son over God's house.

Chapter 4

Jesus is called a great high priest, the Son of God, who has passed through the heavens. He has been tempted in all points as believers are, yet without sin, so he can be touched with the feeling of their infirmities.

Chapter 6

Jesus is described as having entered within the veil as a forerunner for the readers. He has become a high priest forever after the order of Melchizedek.

Chapter 7

Jesus is the Lord who sprang out of Judah, a tribe about which Moses said nothing concerning priesthood, and he was made priest after the power of an endless life and with an oath. He has become the guarantee of a better covenant, lives forever with an unchangeable priesthood, and is able to save to the uttermost those who draw near to God through him. He is holy, guiltless, undefiled, separated from sinners and higher than the heavens, and is the Son perfected forever.

Christ in The Letter to the Hebrews

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ is presented across these records as the Son of God and a high priest named by God, a priest forever after the order of Melchizedek. He did not glorify himself to take this role. In the days of his flesh he prayed with strong crying and tears, was heard for his godly fear, and though a Son learned obedience through suffering. Made perfect, he became the author of eternal salvation for all who obey him. He is also called the author and perfecter of faith, the mediator of a new covenant, the great shepherd of the sheep, and our Lord. The records describe him as the same yesterday, today, and forever. His central work is a single sacrifice. He came as high priest of the coming good things, passed through the greater and more perfect heavenly tabernacle, and entered the Holy Place once for all through his own blood, obtaining eternal redemption. He offered himself without defect to God through the eternal Spirit, in a body prepared to do God's will, to put away sin. Through that one offering he sanctifies and perfects believers forever. He opened a new and living way through the veil of his flesh and is the great priest over God's house. The account unfolds from his appointment and suffering, to his offering and entry into heaven, to his sitting at God's right hand after the one sacrifice and waiting for his enemies to become his footstool. He endured the cross for the joy set before him, despising its shame, and suffered outside the gate to sanctify the people through his blood. God brought him again from the dead. He will appear a second time, not to deal with sin but to save those eagerly waiting for him.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 5

Christ did not glorify himself to become a high priest; God said to him, "You are my Son. Today I have become your father," and called him a priest forever after the order of Melchizedek. In the days of his flesh he offered prayers and petitions with strong crying and tears, was heard for his godly fear, and though a Son learned obedience by what he suffered. Having been made perfect, he became the author of eternal salvation to all who obey him, named by God a high priest after the order of Melchizedek.

Chapter 9

Christ came as high priest of the coming good things and entered the heavenly Holy Place once for all through his own blood, having obtained eternal redemption. He offered himself without defect to God through the eternal Spirit, was revealed once at the end of the ages to put away sin, and will appear a second time to save those eagerly waiting for him.

Chapter 10

Jesus Christ comes into the world with a body prepared for him to do God's will, and through the offering of that body once for all he sanctifies believers. Having offered one sacrifice for sins forever, he sat down at God's right hand, waiting until his enemies become his footstool. By his blood and through the veil of his flesh he opened a new and living way into the holy place, and he is the great priest over God's house.

Chapter 12

Jesus is called the author and perfecter of faith, who for the joy set before him endured the cross, despised its shame, and sat down at the right hand of God's throne. He is also named the mediator of a new covenant, whose blood of sprinkling speaks better than that of Abel. The readers are told to look to him and consider how he endured such contradiction from sinners so they won't grow weary.

Chapter 13

Jesus Christ is described as the same yesterday, today, and forever. He suffered outside the gate to sanctify the people through his own blood, and God brought him again from the dead as the great shepherd of the sheep with the blood of an eternal covenant.

Jesus Christ in 1 Peter

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ is Lord, presented across five chapters of 1 Peter as the central figure of believers' faith. He was foreknown before the foundation of the world and revealed in this last age; his precious blood is likened to that of a lamb without blemish or spot. God raised him from the dead and gave him glory, which gives believers a living hope. He is described as a living stone, rejected by men but chosen by God, and as the chief cornerstone laid in Zion. Sinless and non-retaliating when cursed or abused, he committed himself to the one who judges righteously, and so left believers an example in suffering. He suffered once for sins, the righteous for the unrighteous, bearing sins in his body on the tree to bring believers to God, so they might die to sins and live to righteousness. Put to death in the flesh but made alive in the Spirit, he preached to the spirits in prison who were disobedient in Noah's days. He is now at God's right hand in heaven, with angels, authorities and powers made subject to him. He is called the Shepherd and Overseer of souls, and the chief Shepherd who will be revealed to bring the crown of glory. Believers share his sufferings, will rejoice at the revelation of his glory, and glorify God through him; glory and dominion belong to him forever.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

1 Peter / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is the one whose resurrection from the dead gives believers a living hope, and whose blood sprinkles and redeems them as a lamb without blemish or spot. He was foreknown before the foundation of the world and revealed in this last age, and God raised him from the dead and gave him glory. His sufferings and the glories that would follow were predicted by the Spirit of Christ in the prophets, and his revelation is when believers' faith will result in praise, glory, and honor.

Chapter 2

Jesus Christ is described as a living stone, rejected by men but chosen by God and precious, and as the chief cornerstone in whom believers will not be disappointed. He suffered for believers, leaving an example: he did not sin, no deceit was found in his mouth, he did not curse or threaten when abused, and he committed himself to the one who judges righteously. He bore the sins of believers in his body on the tree, so that they might die to sins and live to righteousness, and his wounds healed them; he is called the Shepherd and Overseer of their souls.

Chapter 3

Christ suffered once for sins, the righteous for the unrighteous, to bring believers to God, being put to death in the flesh but made alive in the Spirit. In the Spirit he went and preached to the spirits in prison who were disobedient in Noah's days. Through his resurrection baptism saves, and he is now at God's right hand in heaven with angels, authorities and powers made subject to him.

Chapter 4

Christ suffered for the believers in the flesh, and his example is the basis for the command to arm themselves with the same mind. Believers share in his sufferings, will rejoice at the revelation of his glory, and glorify God through him; the glory and dominion belong to him forever.

Chapter 5

Christ Jesus is the one by whom God called believers to his eternal glory. The author calls himself a witness of Christ's sufferings, and the closing blessing offers peace to all who are in Christ Jesus. The text also speaks of the chief Shepherd who will be revealed and bring the crown of glory.

Jesus Christ in 1 John

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus Christ is the central figure of the 1 John records, presented as the Son of God and the Son of the Father, with whom believers have fellowship. He is righteous, pure, and without sin, and acts as Counselor with the Father. His blood cleanses those who walk in the light, and he is the atoning sacrifice for the sins of the readers and of the whole world. The account unfolds across five chapters. Early on he is the righteous Counselor and example: those who remain in him ought to walk as he walked, and denying that he is the Christ marks the Antichrist. He was then revealed to take away sins and destroy the devil's works, and he laid down his life for us. Next, confessing that he came in the flesh is the test of a spirit being of God; the Father sent him as Savior of the world. Finally, he is declared the Son of God, who came by water and blood, not water only. Belief in him means being born of God, God's testimony is that eternal life is in him, and he is called the true God and eternal life.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

1 John / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is called the Son of the Father, and the authors' fellowship is with the Father and with him. His blood cleanses those who walk in the light from all sin.

Chapter 2

Jesus Christ is called the righteous Counselor with the Father and the atoning sacrifice for the sins of the readers and of the whole world. Those who say they remain in him ought to walk just as he walked, and the readers are urged to remain in him so they may have boldness when he appears. Denying that Jesus is the Christ marks the Antichrist.

Chapter 3

Jesus Christ, called the Son of God, was revealed to take away sins and to destroy the works of the devil, and no sin is in him. He laid down his life for us, and believing in his name is part of God's commandment.

Chapter 4

Confessing that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is given as the test of whether a spirit is of God. He is the only born Son whom the Father sent into the world as the atoning sacrifice for sins and as the Savior of the world, and whoever confesses him as the Son of God has God remaining in them.

Chapter 5

Jesus Christ is declared to be the Son of God, and whoever believes he is the Christ has been born of God. He came by water and blood, not by water only, and God's testimony concerning him is that eternal life is in him. He is called the true God and eternal life, and believers are in him.

Christ in Revelation

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Christ first appears

Abstract written by a model

Christ, called Jesus Christ, is portrayed in Revelation as the faithful witness, firstborn of the dead, and ruler of the kings of the earth, who loves his people, washed them from sins by his blood, and made them a Kingdom and priests. He is the first and the last, the Living one who was dead and is alive forever, holding the keys of Death and Hades and seven stars in his right hand. In Chapter 1 he is seen as a son of man among lamp stands, coming with the clouds. In Chapter 11 he is the crucified Lord of the two witnesses, and heaven declares the world's kingdom has become his, reigning forever. In Chapter 14, crowned in gold on a white cloud, he reaps the earth with a sharp sickle. In Chapter 20 the faithful live and reign with him for a thousand years as priests.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Revelation / Chapter 1

Jesus Christ is the one whose Revelation God gave, and he is called the faithful witness, the firstborn of the dead, and the ruler of the kings of the earth. He loves and washed his people from their sins by his blood and made them a Kingdom and priests, and he is said to be coming with the clouds so that every eye will see him. John sees him as one like a son of man among the lamp stands, who identifies himself as the first and the last, the Living one who was dead and is alive forever, holding the keys of Death and Hades.

Chapter 11

Christ is referred to as the Lord of the two witnesses, who was crucified in the great city. Voices in heaven declare that the kingdom of the world has become the Kingdom of the Lord and of his Christ, and that he will reign forever and ever.

Chapter 14

One like a son of man is seen sitting on a white cloud, with a golden crown on his head and a sharp sickle in his hand. When an angel from the temple tells him the harvest is ripe, he thrusts his sickle on the earth and the earth is reaped.

Chapter 20

Christ is the one with whom the martyred and faithful souls live and reign for a thousand years. The blessed who share in the first resurrection will be priests of God and of Christ and reign with him.

Jesus in Revelation

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus is presented as the exalted Son of God who speaks to seven assemblies and is described with striking imagery: he holds seven stars in his right hand, walks among seven golden lamp stands, has eyes like fire, feet like burnished brass, and a sharp two-edged sword. In chapters 2 and 3 he addresses each assembly under a different description, such as the first and last who was dead and came to life, the holy and true one with the key of David, and the Faithful and True Witness, the Beginning of God's creation. He claims to know their works and to search minds and hearts. He praises Philadelphia, which he loves and keeps from the hour of testing, rebukes Sardis and Laodicea, reproves and chastens those he loves, threatens judgment on Jezebel and her followers, and promises rewards to overcomers. He stands at the door and knocks, promising to dine with whoever opens, and promises a seat on his throne as he sat with his Father on his throne, having received authority from his Father. In chapter 21 he appears as the Lamb, husband of the bride, the city's wife. The city's foundations bear the names of his twelve Apostles, he is with the Lord God the Almighty its temple and lamp, and his book of life lists who may enter. In chapter 22 he declares he is coming soon with his reward to repay each according to his work, calls himself the Alpha and the Omega, the First and the Last, the Beginning and the End, the root and offspring of David, and the Bright and Morning Star, says he sent his angel to testify to the assemblies, and the text closes with grace for all the saints.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 2

He speaks each letter under a different description: the one who holds the seven stars and walks among the lamp stands, the first and last who was dead and came to life, the one with the sharp two-edged sword, and the Son of God with eyes like fire and feet like burnished brass. He claims to know each assembly's works, to search minds and hearts, and to give to each according to deeds. He threatens to come swiftly, to make war with the sword of his mouth, and to judge Jezebel and her followers, and he promises rewards to those who overcome.

Chapter 3

He addresses Sardis as the one who has the seven Spirits of God and the seven stars, then to Philadelphia as holy and true, holding the key of David, and to Laodicea as the Amen, the Faithful and True Witness, the Beginning of God's creation. He says he knows each assembly's works, rebukes Sardis and Laodicea, praises Philadelphia, and says he is coming quickly. He stands at the door and knocks, promising to dine with whoever opens, and promises overcomers a seat on his throne as he overcame and sat with his Father on his throne.

Chapter 21

The Lamb is called the husband of the bride, as the city is shown as the Lamb's wife. The twelve foundations of the city wall bear the names of his twelve Apostles. Together with the Lord God the Almighty he is the city's temple, he is its lamp, and his book of life lists those who may enter.

Chapter 22

Jesus declares that he is coming soon with his reward to repay each person according to his work, and calls himself the Alpha and the Omega, the First and the Last, the Beginning and the End. He states that he sent his angel to testify these things to the assemblies, and names himself the root and offspring of David and the Bright and Morning Star. The text also refers to him as the Lamb on the throne with God, and closes with "The grace of the Lord Jesus Christ be with all the saints."

Lamb in Revelation

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Lamb first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Lamb is a central figure who appears standing in the middle of the throne, among the four living creatures and elders, looking as though slain, with seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent into all the earth. He is also called the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the Root of David, and Lord of lords and King of kings. He is declared worthy to take the book and open its seals because he was killed and bought people for God with his blood, out of every tribe, language, people, and nation. He receives power, wealth, wisdom, strength, honor, glory, blessing, and worship from the elders, creatures, angels, and all created things. He then opens the first six of the seven seals, and his wrath, with that of the one on the throne, is what earth's people try to hide from. The great multitude praises him alongside God, having washed their robes in his blood, and he shepherds them to springs of life-giving waters. He stands on Mount Zion with the 144,000, who bear his and his Father's names. The ten kings war against him but he overcomes them, and his wedding to his ready wife is announced, with a blessing on those invited to the supper.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 5

The Lamb is seen standing in the middle of the throne, the four living creatures, and the elders, as though slain, with seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent out into all the earth. He takes the book from the right hand of him who sat on the throne. He is declared worthy to take the book and open its seals because he was killed and bought people for God with his blood, and he receives power, wealth, wisdom, strength, honor, glory, and blessing, and worship from the elders, creatures, angels, and all created things.

Chapter 6

The Lamb opens the first six of the seven seals in turn, each opening prompting a living creature's call or a major event. His wrath, together with that of the one on the throne, is what the people of the earth try to hide from.

Chapter 7

The Lamb is praised alongside God by the great multitude, who cry that salvation belongs to God and to the Lamb. His blood is where the multitude washed their robes white, and he stands in the middle of the throne, shepherding them and leading them to springs of life-giving waters.

Chapter 14

The Lamb is seen standing on Mount Zion with the 144,000, who have his name and his Father's name on their foreheads. The 144,000 follow him wherever he goes, and those who worship the beast are tormented in his presence and that of the holy angels.

Chapter 17

The ten kings will war against the Lamb, but the Lamb will overcome them. He is called Lord of lords and King of kings, and those with him are called, chosen, and faithful.

Chapter 19

The wedding of the Lamb is announced as having come, and his wife has made herself ready. A blessing is pronounced on those invited to the wedding supper of the Lamb.

the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary in Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus son of Mary is the Messiah and Allah's messenger. Though some claim to have slain him, he is neither slain nor crucified; Allah takes him up unto Himself. On the Day of Resurrection, he will be a witness against the people of the Scripture.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

153 The people of the Scripture ask of thee that thou shouldst cause an (actual) Book to descend upon them from heaven. They asked a greater thing of Moses aforetime, for they said: Show us Allah plainly. The storm of lightning seized them for their wickedness. Then (even) after that) they chose the calf (for worship) after clear proofs (of Allah's Sovereignty) had come unto them. And We forgave them that! And We bestowed on Moses evident authority. .. 168 Lo! those who disbelieve and deal in wrong, Allah will never forgive them, neither will He guide them unto a road, /

Jesus in Surah 5: The Table (Al-Maaida)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Jesus first appears

Abstract written by a model

Jesus, son of Mary, is strengthened and taught by Allah, speaks in the cradle and maturity, and performs signs by Allah’s permission: shaping a bird, healing the blind and leper, and raising the dead. He worships Allah, curses Israelites who went astray, denies worship of himself and Mary as gods, and witnesses over his people while among them.
